--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is a simple 1-state GoAwayColor with color 4 (yellow), scoring -2.20. This means robots are moving away from yellow objects, which likely pushes them toward the source area (black floor) to pick up items that decrease the score.

Let me think about the mission:
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering one type of item, increases by 1 for the other
- We want to MINIMIZE score, so we want to deliver the GOOD items (score -1 each)
- The source has BLUE (color 3) and GREEN (color 2) walls behind each half
- The nest has RED walls (color 1)
- We need robots to: go to the right source half, pick up items, return to nest

The champion GoAwayColor(yellow=4) works at -2.20. Let me think why - yellow LEDs on robots means robots are repelling each other, spreading them out. Actually, GoAwayColor moves away from color 4 objects... but color 4 is yellow and that's LED color. Actually the color IDs for clr are {1,2,3,4,5,6} which correspond to the wall/floor colors seen by the camera.

Looking at the arena: RED wall (nest side), BLUE walls (one source half), GREEN walls (other source half). The floor colors are WHITE (nest), GRAY (middle), BLACK (source).

GoAwayColor(4=yellow) - yellow might be the robots' own LED color. Moving away from yellow (other robots) causes spreading exploration.

For foraging, a good strategy:
1. Explore to find source (black floor)
2. Go to one specific half (e.g., blue-walled half)
3. Return to nest (white floor / red wall)

Let me design machines:
- State: GoToColor(green=2 or blue=3) to find good source half → when on black floor → GoToColor(red=1) for nest → when on white floor → back to source
- Or: Explore → on black floor → GoToColor(red=1) for nest → on white → explore again

**Strategy**: Go toward one source color (say blue=3), when on black floor go toward red (nest), when on white floor go back to blue source.

FSM designs:

1. **Champion** (survive): GoAwayColor(yellow=4)
2. **Exploit champion**: GoAwayColor(color=5=magenta)
3. **2-state foraging**: GoToColor(blue=3) → on black → GoToColor(red=1) → on white → GoToColor(blue=3)
4. **2-state foraging**: GoToColor(green=2) → on black → GoToColor(red=1) → on white → back
5. **3-state**: Explore → black floor → GoToColor(red=1) → white floor → Explore
6. **3-state**: GoToColor(blue=3) → black → GoAwayColor(blue=3) → white → GoToColor(blue=3)
7. Various explorations

## Reasoning

The champion scores -3.51 with a 3-state machine:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=50) → on BLACK floor (p=0.9) → State 1; on WHITE floor (p=0.8) → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(red=1, nest) → on WHITE floor (p=0.9) → State 1 (self-loop? No, goes to State 1 which is itself... wait)

Let me re-read: `--n1x0 1 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9` means from state 1, transition 0 goes to destination index 1 (among others: [0,2], so D=1 → state 2) on WHITE floor. `--n1x1 0 --c1x1 0 --p1x1 0.05` means destination 0 → state 0 on BLACK floor.

So:
- State 0 (Explore): BLACK→State 1, WHITE→State 1
- State 1 (GoToColor red=1/nest): WHITE→State 2, BLACK→State 0
- State 2 (GoToColor blue=3/source): BLACK→State 1, WHITE→State 0

This is a foraging loop: Explore → find black (source) → go to nest (red wall) → when white (nest) → go to blue source → when black → go to nest again. The key insight: GoToColor(red=1) brings robot to nest, GoToColor(blue=3) brings to one source half.

Now let me think about improvements:
1. The transition from state 0 (explore) on WHITE (p=0.8) also goes to state 1 (GoToColor red). This seems odd - if already at nest, why go to nest? Maybe adjust this.
2. Could try GoToColor(green=2) instead of blue to see if green source is better
3. Add more transitions for robustness
4. Try adjusting probabilities
5. Try 4-state with an additional decision state

Key exploitations:
- Vary rwm0 (50→40, 60)
- Vary transition probabilities
- Try clr2=2 (green) instead of 3 (blue)
- Add neighbor-based conditions
- Try the white-floor transition from state 0 going to state 2 (source) instead of state 1 (nest)

Wait - actually if on WHITE floor you're already at the nest, you should go get items, not go to nest again. Let me reconsider: state 0 WHITE→state 1 (GoToColor red=nest) seems wrong. Maybe it should be WHITE→state 2 (GoToColor blue=source).

Let me design better variants:

**Variant A**: Fix state 0: BLACK→State 1 (nest), WHITE→State 2 (source directly)
**Variant B**: Try green source (clr=2) 
**Variant C**: Add a 4th state for decision making
**Variant D**: Tweak probabilities

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (-16.43) is a 2-state machine: State 0 GoToColor(blue=3) → on BLACK go to State 0 (self... wait, destination 0 among others [1], so D=0 → State 1) on BLACK (p=0.9), on WHITE (p=0.8) → State 1. State 1 GoToColor(red=1) → on WHITE (p=0.9) → State 0, on BLACK (p=0.05) → State 0. So: go to blue source, when on black floor → go to red/nest, when on white floor → back to blue source. This is a clean foraging loop picking up BLUE items (which decrease score).

Green source (clr=2) scored very poorly (+15.66 for the 2-state analog), confirming blue=3 is the good source. Key exploitations: vary probabilities, add a 3rd transition, try adding neighbor conditions, try slightly different probability combos. Also the 3-state with extra gray transition scored -15.04, suggesting exploring that direction too.

## Reasoning

The champion (-16.43) remains stable. All top performers this round are 2-state variants of the champion with slightly different p1x0 values (0.87-0.90) and p1x1 values (0.04-0.06). The 3-state machines consistently underperform the 2-state. I'll focus exploitation on the exact champion topology, sweeping p1x0 in range [0.85-0.92] and p1x1 in [0.03-0.07], while keeping p0x0=0.9 and p0x1=0.8 fixed. A few exploration candidates will try different p0 values and one genuinely different topology.

The sweet spot for p1x0 seems to be around 0.88-0.90, and p1x1 around 0.05-0.06. Values below 0.85 or above 0.92 for p1x0 hurt. I'll sweep finely around these values and also try small changes to p0x0 and p0x1 which have been fixed at 0.9/0.8 throughout - perhaps there's improvement there too.

## Reasoning

The champion (-16.43) uses p1x0=0.9, p1x1=0.05. The nearest competitor (-16.39) uses p1x0=0.87, p1x1=0.06. These are very close, suggesting the optimum is near p1x0≈0.87-0.90, p1x1≈0.05-0.06, p0x0=0.9, p0x1=0.8. Note the champion itself scored -13.68 in this round's re-evaluation, showing stochasticity - the true optimum region is around these values. For the final round I'll densely sample around p1x0∈[0.86,0.90] and p1x1∈[0.05,0.07], keep p0x0=0.9 and p0x1=0.8 fixed (as these have been stable), and include a couple of explorations with slightly varied p0 values.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 13 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.62 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
